Text:"Has GPT-6-Astra Achieved Embodied Intelligence?GPT-6-Astra demonstrates level of competence in robotic manipulation and navigation that challenges the conventional paradigm of building specialized models for individual tasks and embodiments. In this report, we ask not only how capable GPT-6-Astra is, but what its capabilities imply for the field. First, we probe the limits of standalone GPT-6-Astra. We conduct large-scale evaluation across 12 representative benchmarks spanning tabletop manipulation, humanoids, mobile platforms, and dexterous hands. Under unified protocol that allows in-context learning (ICL) from training-set demonstrations and substantially increases the number of execution steps, GPT-6-Astra achieves unprecedented performance  (Fig. 1)  . Our analysis suggests that future research should increasingly focus on improving 1) the generality of in-context learning 2) cost and execution time, 3) physical-state verification, 4) recovery from irreversible failures, and 5) long-horizon preservation of progress. Second, we study the agentic harness for embodied control. Besides exploring the ideal action representation and chucking parameters, we identify that close-loop coordination between high-level "System 2" reasoner (i.e., GPT-6-Astra) and low-level "System 1" controller (e.g., π0.5) is costly but effective. Third, we examine GPT-6-Astra on real-world deployment. Our experiments on 8 robot configurations reveal substantial gaps: 1) robot demonstrations required for incontext learning are expensive to collect, and 2) it is implausible to trade longer execution for higher success rates with physical constraints. Fourth, we envision the next generation of embodied benchmarks. We develop prototype that 1) derives tasks from real-world needs rather than current robot capabilities, 2) provides no in-domain demonstrations, and 3) judges agents on world clock that keeps running while they deliberate. Taken together, our results suggest that GPT-6-Astra has largely crossed the threshold of embodied competence, but not yet that of practical embodied intelligence. The key remaining challenge is to translate benchmark success into reliable, efficient, and scalable real-world deployment. Homepage: https://sensenova.ai/has-gpt-6-astra-achieved-embodied-intelligence Figure 1 Probing the capability upper bound of GPT-6-Astra across 12 benchmarks under unified protocol that 1) allows for in-context learning (ICL) on the training set and 2) extends the step limit (budget) up to 10. RoboLab has no training set and thus no ICL; BEHAVIOR is not extended to 10 due to its large official budget. SOTA cut-off date: October 12, 2026. Details in Tab. 2.The advent of GPT-6-Astra marks major shift in embodied intelligence: general-purpose model can now tackle robotic manipulation and navigation tasks that have traditionally been dominated by specialized models, such as VLAs and WAMs, while exhibiting capabilities including zero-shot generalization, in-context learning, and failure recovery. In this technical report, we use GPT-6-Astra as lens to examine broader question: what does this new level of embodied capability mean for the research community, and where should the field go next? We first conduct large-scale evaluation across diverse embodied tasks to characterize the extent of its capabilities and identify which problems are already approaching saturation. We then study harness design to understand the performancecost trade-offs involved in translating these capabilities into effective control. Next, we validate our findings on real robots, asking how far benchmark success carries over to physical deployment and what gaps remain.Finally, we revisit benchmark design and envision what the next generation of embodied benchmarks should look like. First, we probe the upper bound of GPT-6-Astras embodied capabilities under unified protocol. We conduct large-scale study across 12 representative benchmarks, covering tabletop robotic arms, humanoids, mobile platforms, and dexterous hands. Under this protocol, GPT-6-Astra outperforms existing methods by substantial margins, including recent agentic approaches that also build on GPT-6-Astra. Notably, as shown in Fig. 1, it approaches saturation on the LIBERO series and SIMPLE, while achieving 81.9% on RoboDojo and 87.3% on DexJoCo. The protocol follows two simple and well-motivated principles: 1) We allow in-context learning (ICL) from the training set whenever successful demonstrations are available, which we consider reasonable since such demonstrations are conventionally accessible when training smaller task-specific policies. GPT-6-Astra studies the available training data and constructs frozen knowledge package containing task guidance, motion and visual references, executable helpers, recovery procedures, and metadata. At test time, it retrieves and adapts these resources to the current scene. This substantially improves performance when the demonstrations closely cover the test distribution, but the benefit weakens as tasks move away from the data used to build the package. 2) We extensively increase the execution budget, allowing up to 10 more interaction steps than the official setting. We find that standard step limits can significantly underestimate GPT-6-Astras capabilities. Additional interaction yields large gains on tasks that benefit from repeated correction, such as RoboDojo and Bench2Dex, because GPT-6-Astra can detect failed actions, revise its strategy, and recover through further interaction. This emerging failure-recovery capability is the key mechanism that allows larger execution budget to translate into higher success rates. Our strong empirical results put us in position to identify the remaining limitations of GPT-6-Astra more clearly. Specifically, we find that: i) in-context learning transfer poorly beyond the tasks from which the demonstrations are drawn; ii) even simple tasks incur inference cost and execution time far beyond what would be practical for commercial deployment; iii) GPT-6-Astra often struggles to verify physical success and identify which task condition remains unmet; iv) GPT-6-Astra often struggles to identify the cause of task failure; v) it cannot reliably recover from consequential or irreversible failures; and vi) long-horizon execution remains difficult, as the model struggles to preserve and track progress across many subtasks. Second, we ablate the harness design and find that effectively unl"

Text:"From Research Goals to Scientific Closure: Evaluating Agents Through Evidence-Supported Physics Research From Research Goals to Scientific Closure: Evaluating Agents Through Evidence-Supported Physics Research Yijia Mao1,2* Song Dai1,3* Tao Chen2 Xiangyu Lu2 Linqian Zeng2 Chenxi Hu2 Sitong Zhou2 Dongyang He2 Xiaodan Mao2 Linxuan Zhang2 Tong Zhu1 Yafu Li1 Fangchen Yu1 Peng Ye1 Feng He2 Yu Cheng4 Yun Luo1, 1 Shanghai AI Laboratory 2 Shanghai Jiao Tong University 3 The HongKong University of Science and Technology(Guangzhou) 4 Nanyang Technological University *Equal Contribution, Project Lead, Corresponding Author ABSTRACT Can AI agents organize scientific investigations that lead to well-supported conclusions? To evaluate this capability, we introduce PhysClosureBench, benchmark of 50 expert-verified, goal-specified research tasks spanning strong-field, attosecond, and neighboring physics areas. Each task specifies the research objective and essential scientific constraints, while leaving the agent to organize the investigation and determine how to generate and validate the evidence needed to answer the question. 20-criterion Atomic Score measures local scientific accomplishment, while Research Closure (RC0RC4) assesses how fully the investigation resolves the specified research question based on whether its indispensable evidence requirements and applicable scope conditions are satisfied. We evaluate seven frontier LLMs, where Claude-Opus-5 achieves the highest mean Atomic Score (6.29/10) but reaches RC4 in only 32.7% of attempts. Across the benchmark, 36.2% of attempts scoring at least 8/10 still fall short of RC4, while all seven models reach RC4 on substantially fewer tasks in all three runs than in at least one. Case-level analysis further illustrates how misinterpretation of generated results and unresolved physical applicability limits can lead to incomplete closure. Together, these results reveal persistent gap between AI agents local scientific accomplishment"

Co-last authors. Evaluation has been central to advances in AI, and it is equally critical to autonomous discovery. Yet scaling autonomous discovery to real-world engineering challenges faces fundamental bottleneck: the cost of evaluating progress. In real settings, single true evaluation, such as full benchmark or serving trace, can take hours, so search can afford only handful of true evaluations. We present DuoDiscover, search system that jointly discovers what to optimize and how to evaluate progress efficiently, through three mechanisms. First, each worker in fleet of coding agents co-discovers an optimization target and fast, tailored proxy evaluator, which we call yardstick. The agent then hill-climbs the yardstick rather than the true evaluation. Second, since search can exploit gaps between yardstick and the true objective, fidelity tests continually recheck each yardsticks ranking and invalidate any that drift. Third, the fleet is bottom-up, with no central decomposition of the problem. lightweight orchestrator spawns and terminates agents, briefs running ones, and seeds new agents with others confirmed work. DuoDiscover enables autonomous optimization from software to chip architecture. We demonstrate DuoDiscover on two repository-level benchmarks and four GPU and TPU kernel suites covering hundreds of kernels. On SWE-fficiency, it doubles the average speedup of human experts. On SOL-Exec, it ranks first on 121 of 23"

Text:"Dream2Discern: Learning from Hard Latent Variants for Runtime Failure Prediction in Multi-Agent Systems Boxuan Zhang1 Jianing Zhu2 Xiaotian Han3 Ruixiang Tang1, 1 Rutgers University 2 The University of Texas at Austin 3 Reliable deployment of LLM-based multi-agent systems calls for runtime auditors that predict terminal outcomes from observed execution prefixes. These auditors typically learn from limited corpus of trajectories with verified outcomes. Expanding this corpus requires costly execution and verification, yet broader failure coverage alone provides no explicit criterion for identifying prefixes that the current auditor finds difficult to classify. To this end, we propose Dream2Discern, runtime auditor trained through implicit augmentation guided by its own prediction loss. During Dream, we select perturbations of the auditors representation operators to increase prediction loss on recorded prefixes, yielding hard latent variants. During Discern, the auditor learns to predict execution outcomes from these variants using the original labels. The variants are regenerated as the auditor learns, turning limited corpus into renewable source of hard examples and requiring no additional trajectory collection or outcome verification. Extensive evaluations on AFTraj-2K and StepShield show that Dream2Discern outperforms competitive failure auditors and achieves over 90% trajectory-level weighted accuracy on both benchmarks while balancing prediction accuracy and timeliness. Text:"Embeddings Are Overpriced? Frozen 16 MB Vocabulary for Small and Ternary Language Models Sai Kiran Bathula QLNI, New South Wales, Australia saikiranbathula@qlni.io Abstract In small language model the vocabulary is the single largest part. In Gemma 3 270M the embedding table holds 168M of 268M parameters, and because quantisation methods leave the embedding in 16-bit, it is also most of the file once the rest of the model is compressed. We ask what that table actually buys. We replace it with one frozen table of 512-bit codes, 16 MB, with no trainable parameters, derived once from pretrained embedding by principal-component projection and sign binarisation, and we train the same Gemma-shaped body from scratch on the same 2.05B tokens with and without it. The learned table buys 0.20 nats of validation loss for 2.6 times the trainable parameters, but on LAMBADA, HellaSwag and PIQA the frozen-table model is better or tied; only ARC-Easy favours the learned table. With ternary BitNet b1.58 body the whole model is 46 MB file, still ties the float model on LAMBADA and PIQA, and on laptop CPU generates 615 to 740 tokens per second in 62 MB of memory, five to six times the speed of the float models 8-bit GGUF in llama.cpp at seventh of the memory. The frozen table wins by 1.7 to 2.3 nats on rare words, accepts 10,000 new words after training with 3% cost where the learned table collapses by 43%, and all of it is released with the weights, the data and every evaluation answer. Code, weights of every arm (including intermediate checkpoints), training data, logs and per-question evaluation results: huggingface.co/QLNI/embeddings-are-overpriced. Preprint DOI: doi.org/10.5281/zenodo.23382634. Released for non-commercial research. Three things happened to small language models in the last few years. Text:"PremiseCheck: Efficient Fact-Checking of Human and AI Peer Reviews Dingjie Song1, Zhiling Yan1, Ziqian Zhang1, Yuxuan Zhang2,3, Lichao Sun1, 1Lehigh University, 2University of British Columbia, 3Vector Institute Corresponding author Peer review is under strain from the growth of submissions, and review comments written by humans or AI can misstate what paper says. We introduce ReviewFact, benchmark that labels such factual errors in review comments under one definition across NLP, machine learning, and the natural sciences, and that measures cost and latency alongside accuracy. frontier LLM checks comments well but must read the whole paper for each check, while cheap decision models, which score whether statement holds in text, fall far short on whole comments that mix several implicit assertions. PremiseCheck is framework that combines the two. An LLM rewrites each comment as explicit premises without reading the paper, and decision model checks each premise against the paper. When each comment is checked on its own, PremiseCheck reaches accuracy comparable to GPT-5.5 across all three fields at about half its cost, and on NLP reviews at quarter of its cost and less than half its latency. Code: https://github.com/OpenLAIR/PremiseCheck Data: https://huggingface.co/datasets/songdj/ReviewFact Website: https://openlair.github.io/premisecheck/ The number of research papers has grown explosively in recent years, and reviewing them now places heavy burden on the scientific community (Shah, 2022; Kuznetsov et al., 2024; Kim et al., 2025). review comment is useful only if what it says about the paper is true, yet factual errors appear regularly in both human-written and AI-written comments (Du et al., 2024; Ryu et al., 2025; Kim et al., 2026), and AI systems now help write growing share of reviews (Liang et al., 2024a; Thakkar et al., 2025; Biswas et al., 2026). Such errors mislead authors and distort decisions, and at the present scale they can no longer be caught by hand."

Text:"2026-09-29 FC-SWE: Failure-Conditioned RL for Long-Horizon Software Engineering Agents Jia Liufu1,2, Bin Hu1, Linglin Jing1, Terry Kong1, Yuki Huang1, Ashwath Aithal1, Wenming Yang2 and Jun Yang1 1NVIDIA, 2Tsinghua University Abstract: Repository-level software engineering (SWE) is challenging long-horizon setting: agents must reason over extended interactions, use tools across multiple turns, and adapt to observations from stateful environments. Recent work trains SWE agents with reinforcement learning methods such as Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO), which independently sample multiple trajectories per issue, test the resulting patches, and compare terminal rewards within fixed group. However, this training setup does not reuse verifier feedback from failed patches as context for subsequent attempts, even though this feedback contains valuable diagnostic information about what went wrong. Training on recovery trajectories is challenging because the preceding outcome determines whether the next trajectory is generated, while the failed execution determines its conditioning context. We introduce FC-SWE, failure-conditioned RL framework that incorporates recovery attempts into policy training. After patch fails verification, FC-SWE restores the repository to its original task state and uses the failed patch and verifier feedback as context for recovery trajectory. FC-SWE adapts GRPO to these chains of complete, multi-turn tool-use trajectories through two mechanisms. Trajectory-local rewards preserve each attempts verifier outcome, preventing recovery success from rewarding an earlier failed patch. Active-set advantage estimation forms comparison group from all initial and recovery trajectories actually executed for the same issue, so failed attempts remain in the group while unexecuted attempts are excluded. On all 500 SWE-bench Verified tasks under verifier-assisted protocol, FC-SWE with Qwen3.5-4B and SWE-agent achieves 41.7% Resolved@1 and 52.8% Resolved"

Text:"LEV: Thinking Fast and Deep with Looped Decision Models Dingjie Song1,, Zhiling Yan1,, Yuxuan Zhang2,3, Lichao Sun1, 1Lehigh University, 2University of British Columbia, 3Vector Institute Equal contribution, Corresponding author Decision models answer typed questions about an input with one probability per option, computed in single forward pass without generating text. Like the fast System 1 of dual-process theory, they answer at once, but their depth is fixed at training time, so they spend the same computation on easy and hard questions. Models that reason in generated text can think longer, but they pay for every extra step in decoded tokens. We present LEV, decision model that thinks deeper by rerunning its layers in loop instead of generating tokens. LEV builds on language model pretrained as loop, reads the options after every loop step and trains all steps jointly, so one checkpoint can answer at any depth. On three looped backbones, accuracy rises with depth on the same weights, mostly on hard questions, and 2.6B LEV goes from 63.2% at depth one to 76.2% at depth four on JevBench. On-demand depth, set per question by small stopper with threshold fitted on unlabelled requests, saves 15% to 24% of the computation at cost of under one accuracy point, level with or slightly above fixed mix of depths. On locked test set, the 1.4B and 3B models are 5.4 to 8.8 points more accurate than dense (non-looped) models and cascades of the same latency below 50 ms. Code: https://github.com/OpenLAIR/lev Models: https://huggingface.co/collections/songdj/lev Website: https://openlair.github.io/lev/ Generative language models that reason with chain-of-thought act as System 2 (Kahneman, 2011). Abstract GPT-6-Astra achieves high performance across diverse robotic tasks via in-context learning, yet real-world deployment reveals gaps in demonstration cost, execution time, and practical embodied intelligence.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small GPT-6-Astra demonstrates a level of competence in robotic manipulation and navigation that challenges the conventional paradigm of building specialized models for individual tasks and embodiments. In this report, we ask not only how capable GPT-6-Astra is, but what its capabilities imply for the field. First, we probe the limits of standalone GPT-6-Astra. We conduct a large-scale evaluation across 12 representative benchmarks spanning tabletop manipulation, humanoids, mobile platforms, and dexterous hands. Under a unified protocol that allows in-context learning (ICL) from training-set demonstrations and substantially increases the number of execution steps, GPT-6-Astra achieves unprecedented performance (Fig. 1). Future research may focus on improving 1) the generality of ICL 2) cost and execution time, 3) physical-state verification, 4) recovery from irreversible failures, and 5) long-horizon preservation of progress. Second, we study the agentic harness for embodied control. We identify that close-loop coordination between a high-level "System 2" reasoner (i.e., GPT-6-Astra) and a low-level "System 1" controller (e.g., π0.5) is costly but effective. Third, we examine GPT-6-Astra on real-world deployment. Our experiments on 8 robot configurations reveal substantial gaps: 1) robot demonstrations required for ICL are expensive to collect, and 2) it is implausible to trade longer execution for higher success rates with physical constraints. Fourth, we envision the next generation of embodied benchmarks. We develop a prototype that 1) derives tasks from real-world needs, 2) provides no in-domain demonstrations, and 3) judges agents on a world clock that keeps running while they deliberate. Taken together, our results suggest that GPT-6-Astra has largely crossed the threshold of embodied competence, but not yet that of practical embodied intelligence.

Abstract HexaShell is a coding agent that controls a dual-arm robot via vision and self-written tools, achieving state-of-the-art results on RoboDojo but struggling with contact-heavy tasks.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Agents built on vision language models (VLMs) now compete with learned policies on robot manipulation leaderboards. We ask how far a coding agent can go when it controls a robot instead of a computer, and where it stops. Our system, HexaShell, keeps the harness thin: it only turns the agent's commands into robot motion. The agent drives a robot with two arms through one command line. It sees three RGB cameras and knows their calibration. After each failed attempt, the agent reads the log, finds the cause, and writes or fixes small programs called tools. In this way it keeps what it learns as code, as a coding agent does when it fixes a failing test. The tools may use only what the robot observes. They use triangulation to find where objects are, relative to each other and to the gripper. If two cameras see the same point, the point lies where the two camera rays meet. If a point lies on a known plane, such as the table or the top of a block, it lies where its ray meets that plane. When the head camera is not enough, the robot moves a wrist camera to get a second view. We build the tools on only ten layouts of one seed per task, then freeze them and test them on ten new layouts of the same seed. We use Codex with GPT-6-Astra at medium reasoning effort. On all 42 RoboDojo tasks, HexaShell reaches a success rate of 39.7% and a progress score of 48.0, averaged in the same way as the leaderboard. This is a new state of the art on RoboDojo. The limit is contact, not reasoning: pushing reaches 92.5% and memory tasks 65.0%, but tight insertion only 8.3%. When the agent fails, it usually sees the failure but cannot make the motion work. We open source the tools evolved so far at https://github.com/HexaFuture/HexaShell.

Paper text to classify:

"Abstract HexaShell is a coding agent that controls a dual-arm robot via vision and self-written tools, achieving state-of-the-art results on RoboDojo but struggling with contact-heavy tasks.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Agents built on vision language models (VLMs) now compete with learned policies on robot manipulation leaderboards. We ask how far a coding agent can go when it controls a robot instead of a computer, and where it stops. Our system, HexaShell, keeps the harness thin: it only turns the agent's commands into robot motion. The agent drives a robot with two arms through one command line. It sees three RGB cameras and knows their calibration. After each failed attempt, the agent reads the log, finds the cause, and writes or fixes small programs called tools. In this way it keeps what it learns as code, as a coding agent does when it fixes a failing test. The tools may use only what the robot observes. They use triangulation to find where objects are, relative to each other and to the gripper. If two cameras see the same point, the point lies where the two camera rays meet. If a point lies on a known plane, such as the table or the top of a block, it lies where its ray meets that plane. When the head camera is not enough, the robot moves a wrist camera to get a second view. We build the tools on only ten layouts of one seed per task, then freeze them and test them on ten new layouts of the same seed. We use Codex with GPT-6-Astra at medium reasoning effort. On all 42 RoboDojo tasks, HexaShell reaches a success rate of 39.7% and a progress score of 48.0, averaged in the same way as the leaderboard. This is a new state of the art on RoboDojo. The limit is contact, not reasoning: pushing reaches 92.5% and memory tasks 65.0%, but tight insertion only 8.3%. When the agent fails, it usually sees the failure but cannot make the motion work. We open source the tools evolved so far at https://github.com/HexaFuture/HexaShell."

Abstract ACT3D trains vision-language models via reinforcement learning to actively explore 3D environments from single first-person views, improving answer accuracy through adaptive, question-conditioned navigation without direct exploration supervision.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small We study how vision-language models (VLMs) can learn to think and actively explore in 3D space. Instead of providing multi-view images, videos, or 3D representations to the model, we preserve the native visual encoding of VLMs, i.e., a single image, one at a time, just like humans, and treat the 3D representation only as an environment with which the model can interact through actions (e.g., move left) and observations (rendered images). Each rollout begins from an initial first-person view and a visual question. The model can then iteratively reason, navigate, and reorient its viewpoint before committing to a final answer. Following an initial trajectory-supervised finetuning, we apply reinforcement learning (RL) with rewards driven primarily by final-answer correctness. Crucially, RL provides no direct supervision over which viewpoints the model should visit or which exploration actions it should take. Instead, the model must learn an effective exploration policy solely from the extent to which its interactions improve performance. Across six 3D benchmarks, our model ACT3D achieves an average accuracy of 53.2% that improves its base Qwen model by 22.0% and surpasses proprietary Gemini 3.6 Flash. ACT3D also outperforms existing open-source 3D methods while using 50-70% fewer input frames, and generalizes to unseen domains with an average improvement of 5.9% over its supervised initialization. Further analysis reveals adaptive, question-conditioned exploration. Performance gains over the base model increase from 0.6% on questions answerable from the initial view to 16.3% on those requiring extensive exploration. ACT3D searches for occluded targets, avoids unnecessary exploration, and acquires complementary viewpoints for spatial comparisons. These findings demonstrate that outcome-based RL enables VLMs not only to reason over visual evidence, but also to learn when and how to acquire it.
[19.10.2026 08:24] Response: {"desc": "В статье представлен ACT3D — визуально-языковая модель (VLM), которая учится активно исследовать 3D-пространство, получая на вход лишь одно изображение от первого лица, как это делают люди. Модель сначала проходит supervised finetuning на траекториях, а затем обучается с помощью reinforcement learning, где награда зависит только от правильности финального ответа, без прямой разметки того, какие ракурсы посещать. Abstract BridgeWAM decouples video and action experts via latent bridge queries to improve action prediction efficiency and generalization in world action models.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small World Action Models (WAMs) commonly couple video and action experts through dense, layer-wise Mixture-of-Transformers (MoT) attention, exposing action prediction to irrelevant visual features while enforcing matched expert depths. This coupling hinders selective visual-feature use and introduces redundant computation. We introduce Bridge-of-Experts (BoE), instantiated as BridgeWAM, which aggregates visual, language, and proprioceptive priors through 32 Latent Bridge Queries (LBQs) into a shared, limited latent space. Joint video-action supervision lets action gradients guide the Video Expert toward representations better suited for action prediction, while the Action Expert learns to selectively retrieve task-relevant information through cross-attention. Decoupling expert depths and reusing LBQs during action denoising yield a 5.06B model with a 30-layer Video Expert and a two-layer Action Expert, achieving 98.25% success on LIBERO and 72.24% pooled success on LIBERO-Plus, demonstrating effective task execution and strong generalization with an ultralight action head. Ablations support joint representation adaptation and unified LBQ conditioning. Relative to Fast-WAM, BridgeWAM reduces single-sample inference FLOPs by 28.72% in simulation profiling and real-robot latency to 77.1 ms (a 2.47-fold speedup). Qualitative experimental results further indicate preferential attention to target objects and task destinations across perturbations.

Paper text to classify:

"Abstract BridgeWAM decouples video and action experts via latent bridge queries to improve action prediction efficiency and generalization in world action models.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small World Action Models (WAMs) commonly couple video and action experts through dense, layer-wise Mixture-of-Transformers (MoT) attention, exposing action prediction to irrelevant visual features while enforcing matched expert depths. This coupling hinders selective visual-feature use and introduces redundant computation. We introduce Bridge-of-Experts (BoE), instantiated as BridgeWAM, which aggregates visual, language, and proprioceptive priors through 32 Latent Bridge Queries (LBQs) into a shared, limited latent space. Joint video-action supervision lets action gradients guide the Video Expert toward representations better suited for action prediction, while the Action Expert learns to selectively retrieve task-relevant information through cross-attention. Decoupling expert depths and reusing LBQs during action denoising yield a 5.06B model with a 30-layer Video Expert and a two-layer Action Expert, achieving 98.25% success on LIBERO and 72.24% pooled success on LIBERO-Plus, demonstrating effective task execution and strong generalization with an ultralight action head. Ablations support joint representation adaptation and unified LBQ conditioning. Relative to Fast-WAM, BridgeWAM reduces single-sample inference FLOPs by 28.72% in simulation profiling and real-robot latency to 77.1 ms (a 2.47-fold speedup). Qualitative experimental results further indicate preferential attention to target objects and task destinations across perturbations."

Abstract OpenMobile-2 is an open mobile agent trained with diverse simulated apps, large-scale supervised and reinforcement learning data, and hybrid GUI-tool use, achieving strong benchmark results and real-device transfer.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small We introduce OpenMobile-2, a near-frontier mobile agent with fully open training environments and recipes. We make three key advances: (1) Diverse environments with simulated commercial apps: We build MobileGym++, featuring 35 realistic, functionally rich commercial-style apps with cross-app workflows, while preserving full controllability for reset and verification. Together with newly configured apps in Android emulators, this yields a diverse playground spanning over 110 apps. (2) Open training data at scale: Building on this foundation, we curate nearly 12K mobile interaction trajectories for supervised fine-tuning and the largest open collection of verifiable RL training data for mobile agents, comprising over 2K executable tasks with automatic rewards. (3) Hybrid GUI and app-native tool use: We explore an experimental mobile-use setting where apps expose selected functionalities as app-native tools alongside their GUIs, allowing agents to interleave GUI actions and tool calls within a task. We implement this setting in MobileGym++ with over 300 carefully scoped tools across 50+ apps. Additionally, we introduce MobileGym++ Bench for complex, long-horizon commercial mobile scenarios, supporting both GUI-only and hybrid GUI-tool evaluation on a shared task suite. OpenMobile-2 performs competitively across established benchmarks, including AndroidWorld (79.9) and MobileWorld (50.4), while showing promising transfer to real-device mobile use, nearly doubling SPA-Bench performance from 31.9 to 59.6. All resources are available at https://os-copilot.github.io/OpenMobile2-Home/.

Abstract AI agents evaluated on physics research tasks show strong local scientific results but frequently fail to fully resolve research questions due to incomplete evidence and unresolved applicability limits.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Can AI agents organize scientific investigations that lead to well-supported conclusions? To evaluate this capability, we introduce PhysClosureBench, a benchmark of 50 expert-verified, goal-specified research tasks spanning strong-field, attosecond, and neighboring physics areas. Each task specifies the research objective and essential scientific constraints, while leaving the agent to organize the investigation and determine how to generate and validate the evidence needed to answer the question. A 20-criterion Atomic Score measures local scientific accomplishment, while Research Closure (RC0--RC4) assesses how fully the investigation resolves the specified research question based on whether its indispensable evidence requirements and applicable scope conditions are satisfied. We evaluate seven frontier LLMs, where Claude-Opus-5 achieves the highest mean Atomic Score (6.29/10) but reaches RC4 in only 32.7% of attempts. Across the benchmark, 36.2% of attempts scoring at least 8/10 still fall short of RC4, while all seven models reach RC4 on substantially fewer tasks in all three runs than in at least one. Case-level analysis further illustrates how misinterpretation of generated results and unresolved physical applicability limits can lead to incomplete closure. Together, these results reveal a persistent gap between AI agents' local scientific accomplishments and their ability to achieve complete research closure.

"Abstract AI agents evaluated on physics research tasks show strong local scientific results but frequently fail to fully resolve research questions due to incomplete evidence and unresolved applicability limits.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Can AI agents organize scientific investigations that lead to well-supported conclusions? To evaluate this capability, we introduce PhysClosureBench, a benchmark of 50 expert-verified, goal-specified research tasks spanning strong-field, attosecond, and neighboring physics areas. Each task specifies the research objective and essential scientific constraints, while leaving the agent to organize the investigation and determine how to generate and validate the evidence needed to answer the question. A 20-criterion Atomic Score measures local scientific accomplishment, while Research Closure (RC0--RC4) assesses how fully the investigation resolves the specified research question based on whether its indispensable evidence requirements and applicable scope conditions are satisfied. We evaluate seven frontier LLMs, where Claude-Opus-5 achieves the highest mean Atomic Score (6.29/10) but reaches RC4 in only 32.7% of attempts. Across the benchmark, 36.2% of attempts scoring at least 8/10 still fall short of RC4, while all seven models reach RC4 on substantially fewer tasks in all three runs than in at least one. Case-level analysis further illustrates how misinterpretation of generated results and unresolved physical applicability limits can lead to incomplete closure. Together, these results reveal a persistent gap between AI agents' local scientific accomplishments and their ability to achieve complete research closure."

Abstract Speculative Sampling enables interactive video generation with large bidirectional windows by caching predictions and delivering them incrementally, reducing latency without sacrificing quality.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Interactive video generation commonly relies on small bidirectional chunks to reduce response latency. Yet our preliminary experiments show that larger chunks improve video quality and better utilize GPU parallelism, allowing more latents to be sampled with only a modest increase in sampling time. Motivated by these observations, we propose Speculative Sampling, a strategy that combines the modeling power of large bidirectional windows with the low-latency interaction of small ones. It expands the modeling window by appending noise latents as padding and caches the resulting predictions for reuse when the condition remains unchanged. Predictions are delivered incrementally, while the uncommitted future remains replaceable. Our latency analysis shows that this removes the waiting-time lower bound imposed by chunk boundaries, shifting the optimization focus from reducing chunk size to accelerating per-chunk sampling. Bidirectional window size thus becomes a decision variable for jointly optimizing visual quality and sampling time, rather than a necessary constraint on low-latency interaction. This opens a path to serving large bidirectional models in interactive streaming systems without complex streaming post-training.

Идея в том, что модель дополняет окно шумовыми latent-векторами как padding, а предсказания кэшируются и выдаются инкрементально, при этом непоздно зафиксированное будущее можно заменить. Анализ задержки показывает, что это снимает нижнюю границу ожидания, связанную с границами чанков, и смещает оптимизацию на ускорение сэмплирования каждого чанка. Abstract DLite improves speculative decoding for agentic AI by eliminating historical context maintenance and using target hidden states for local predictions, boosting acceptance length and reducing overhead.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Speculative decoding has become a mainstream technique for accelerating large language model (LLM) inference by using a lightweight draft model to generate candidate tokens that are subsequently verified by the target model. However, agentic workloads introduce long and continuously evolving contexts and frequent multi-turn interactions. Under such workloads, we observe that existing draft models suffer from up to an 80% degradation in acceptance length, while maintaining their historical KV cache introduces substantial system overhead. In this paper, we identify the growing historical context as a common source of both problems and introduce DLite, a history-free speculative decoding framework for agentic AI. DLite directly reuses contextual hidden states from the target model and performs local speculative prediction without explicitly maintaining the historical context, transforming an increasingly complex modeling problem into local simple prediction problem. A lightweight sequential head further restores intra-block causal dependencies with low overhead. To effectively train this heterogeneous history-free architecture, we introduce a tailored two-stage training strategy. Experiments on general-purpose, long-context and agentic workloads show that DLite achieves 2-6 acceptance length and outperforms the state-of-the-art speculative decoding method, DSpark, up to 70%.
[19.10.2026 08:25] Response: {"desc": "Статья представляет DLite — фреймворк для speculative decoding в agentic AI, который не поддерживает историческое KV-кэширование, а использует скрытые состояния target-модели для локальных предсказаний. Авторы показывают, что существующие draft-модели теряют до 80% acceptance length на длинных многоходовых контекстах, а поддержка истории создаёт значительные системные издержки. Легковесная sequential head восстанавливает причинные зависимости внутри блока при низких накладных расходах, а двухэтапная стратегия обучения позволяет эффективно тренировать такую гетерогенную архитектуру. Abstract RAP introduces a large-scale dataset and benchmark for generating conference posters from papers as a visual coding task, providing verified paper-poster-markup triplets and both retrieval-based and trained generation methods.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Making a conference poster is visual coding in miniature: an entire paper must be compressed onto a single page, which exercises summarization, visual design, and code implementation at once, using nothing beyond the paper itself. Yet benchmarks for this task in the context of visual coding remain scarce: existing resources are small and annotated only with layout boxes, so evaluation must fall back on VLM-as-judge or human checklists rather than objective render-and-compare, and the generation methods they support design layouts from scratch, leaving human-crafted posters unusable as either a retrievable design library or as verified supervision. We introduce RAP (Reconstruct A Poster), a dataset and benchmark for paper-to-poster generation as a visual coding task. RAP contains 25K+ verified paper-poster pairs from various machine learning conferences held between 2022 and 2026, annotated with a three-level content taxonomy, plus a held-out benchmark of 150 test papers each paired with its human poster. A fully automated derendering pipeline turns each poster into styled HTML/CSS of typed, refillable slots, so every sample is a paper-poster-markup triplet verifiable by rendering against the source poster. The corpus serves both inference and training: PosterRAG, a training-free multi-agent pipeline, retrieves an exemplar and refills its slots without touching layout or styling, while an SFT+RL recipe trains a dedicated model from the same triplets. An online demo is available at https://reconstruct-a-poster.github.io/.

Датасет содержит более 25 тысяч проверенных пар «статья–постер» с конференций по машинному обучению (2022–2026), а также тестовые примеры с человеческими постерами. Постеры автоматически переводятся в HTML/CSS с заполняемыми слотами, что позволяет верифицировать результат рендерингом. Предложены два подхода: PosterRAG, не требующий обучения, который извлекает похожий шаблон и заполняет его содержимым, и модель, обученную через SFT+RL на этих данных.", "emoji": "🖼️", "title": "От статьи к постеру: генерация научных постеров как визуальный кодинг"}

Abstract Lexical reference frames interpret hidden states through a model's own remaining layers, enabling accurate attribution, steering, and early layer exit.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small The vocabulary of a language model is its native semantics: the embedding and the unembedding fix the meaning of a state at the two ends of the residual stream. Between them, existing methods either reuse a fixed lens or treat the states as a point cloud, and neither reads a state the way the model does. We argue that every hidden state has a native reference frame, induced by the layers that read it next and adaptive to the context. Lexical reference frames approximate it by reading a state through the model's own remaining layers with a stated number of rounds of attention between positions and a typical attention output for the rest, without fitting to the model's output. No lens, with fixed directions per layer, can exceed the square root of the native frame's coherence, 0.30 and 0.35 at mid-depth of SmolLM3-3B and OLMo3-7B, whereas the first-order frame, LexFrame[1], reaches 0.97 and 0.97 and reads a state within 0.16 nats of the model's output. Its coefficients attribute predictions more faithfully than attribution patching, its lexical basis steers a prediction while sparing other tokens, and its confident readings would let an early exit skip 0.45 and 0.42 of the layers, where lenses skip almost none. In this way, lexical reference frames make every hidden state of the model readable, attributable and steerable.
[19.10.2026 08:25] Response: {"desc": "Статья предлагает понятие «лексического опорного фрейма» (lexical reference frame), который интерпретирует скрытое состояние LLM через оставшиеся слои самой модели, а не через фиксированный lens. Метод LexFrame[1] почти точно воспроизводит выход модели (расхождение 0.16 nats) и превосходит существующие lens-методы по когерентности. На его основе можно точнее атрибутировать предсказания, управлять генерацией конкретных токенов и делать early exit, пропуская около 45% слоёв. Abstract MIST separates scene setup from change execution to isolate memory failures in video world models, and M3-Agent improves instruction execution to significantly boost benchmark scores.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small A memory test for a video world model should say whether the scene was set, whether the asked change was carried out, and whether the last frame shows the result. Current tests fold these into one number, so a low score is easy to read as forgetting. When the change is not carried out, the hidden state transition cannot appear, so the test is not really a test of memory. We introduce MIST, an evaluation that separates the change from the memory it should leave behind. The model first sets the scene while the change stays hidden, and only later is asked to show it, with leakage before that point kept under control. This separation locates the bottleneck: the model sets the scene but does not carry out the instructed change. The same gap appears in general memory evaluations. On that basis we propose M3-Agent (Make the Missing Move), which recursively revises the details of the input instruction so that the model successfully carries out the requested instruction. Relative to the baseline, it more than triples the MIST reveal score. On the benchmarks, WRBench's score T rises by 26.1% and MBench-T's score U by 52.7%. The method meets the problem from the execution side: it shows what the frozen model can do once the move is stated, and it sets a stricter baseline.

Paper text to classify:

"Abstract MIST separates scene setup from change execution to isolate memory failures in video world models, and M3-Agent improves instruction execution to significantly boost benchmark scores.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small A memory test for a video world model should say whether the scene was set, whether the asked change was carried out, and whether the last frame shows the result. Current tests fold these into one number, so a low score is easy to read as forgetting. When the change is not carried out, the hidden state transition cannot appear, so the test is not really a test of memory. We introduce MIST, an evaluation that separates the change from the memory it should leave behind. The model first sets the scene while the change stays hidden, and only later is asked to show it, with leakage before that point kept under control. This separation locates the bottleneck: the model sets the scene but does not carry out the instructed change. The same gap appears in general memory evaluations. On that basis we propose M3-Agent (Make the Missing Move), which recursively revises the details of the input instruction so that the model successfully carries out the requested instruction. Relative to the baseline, it more than triples the MIST reveal score. On the benchmarks, WRBench's score T rises by 26.1% and MBench-T's score U by 52.7%. The method meets the problem from the execution side: it shows what the frozen model can do once the move is stated, and it sets a stricter baseline."

Abstract OfficeTown is a generative simulation framework that creates persistent professional organizations to generate work-grounded training data and evaluation benchmarks for AI agents.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small AI agents increasingly use files and software tools to produce professional deliverables. Such work draws on materials, decisions, and feedback accumulated across assignments. We introduce OfficeTown, a generative simulation framework for professional organizations in which tasks and their context arise from continuing organizational work. From a natural-language description of an organization's purpose and activities, OfficeTown initializes an organization whose members coordinate work, delegate bounded assignments to assistant agents, and review their deliverables. Artifacts, decisions, and unresolved matters persist and shape subsequent work, while responsibilities and permissions determine what each member can see and do. We curate work requests, their context, and review feedback into executable resources for evaluation and learning. We construct OfficeTown Bench with 406 tasks and 4,836 evaluation criteria from 23 simulated organizations across eight professional domains, incorporating domain-expert feedback. Frontier-model evaluations reveal substantial remaining headroom. Supervised fine-tuning and on-policy distillation on OfficeTown data substantially improve Qwen3.5-9B on external professional-work benchmarks, including GDPval. Supplementary evaluations show that the principal--assistant harness achieves higher scores than the single- and multi-agent baselines tested. These results highlight the value of organizational simulation in generating work-grounded data and environments for the continued development of generalist agents.
[19.10.2026 08:25] Response: {"desc": "Статья представляет OfficeTown — фреймворк генеративной симуляции, который создаёт постоянно существующие профессиональные организации с сотрудниками, артефактами и решениями. На основе этих симуляций авторы формируют обучающие данные и бенчмарк OfficeTown Bench из 406 задач и 4836 критериев оценки для AI-агентов. Дообучение (SFT и on-policy distillation) на этих данных заметно улучшает модель Qwen3.5-9B на внешних бенчмарках, включая GDPval. Abstract SciΠvot evaluates LLM agents' scientific judgment using 248 human-corrected cases, revealing that frontier models often prefer flawed plans over expert corrections and perform below human levels.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Large language model (LLM) agents can keep scientific experiments running for months with little human supervision, but reliable autonomous research also requires scientific judgment. Evaluating scientific judgment is difficult because research decisions are open-ended and depend on project context. We construct SciΠvot, a 248-case benchmark of scientific judgment based on human corrections. The benchmark draws on records from six months of research in which senior researchers ran LLM agents on 26 projects across six scientific fields and repeatedly intervened to correct the agents' research judgments. In each case, an agent chooses between the original LLM plan and the human-corrected alternative, using only the project record available before the correction as context. We compare LLM and human performance and examine the effects of reasoning effort, access to the project record, and reconsideration. At medium reasoning effort with the project record, models systematically prefer the original LLM plan over the human-corrected alternative. Four of five frontier models perform below chance on the selected cases. The highest accuracy in the project condition across the tested models and reasoning effort settings is 63.5%, compared with 73.4% for fresh researchers. At medium effort, every model achieves lower accuracy with the project record than without it, and reconsideration does not consistently improve overall accuracy. Even with a human-corrected alternative available, frontier models struggle with the selected research decisions, while SciΠvot supports systematic evaluation of scientific judgment for LLM agents.

"Abstract SciΠvot evaluates LLM agents' scientific judgment using 248 human-corrected cases, revealing that frontier models often prefer flawed plans over expert corrections and perform below human levels.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Large language model (LLM) agents can keep scientific experiments running for months with little human supervision, but reliable autonomous research also requires scientific judgment. Evaluating scientific judgment is difficult because research decisions are open-ended and depend on project context. We construct SciΠvot, a 248-case benchmark of scientific judgment based on human corrections. The benchmark draws on records from six months of research in which senior researchers ran LLM agents on 26 projects across six scientific fields and repeatedly intervened to correct the agents' research judgments. In each case, an agent chooses between the original LLM plan and the human-corrected alternative, using only the project record available before the correction as context. We compare LLM and human performance and examine the effects of reasoning effort, access to the project record, and reconsideration. At medium reasoning effort with the project record, models systematically prefer the original LLM plan over the human-corrected alternative. Four of five frontier models perform below chance on the selected cases. The highest accuracy in the project condition across the tested models and reasoning effort settings is 63.5%, compared with 73.4% for fresh researchers. At medium effort, every model achieves lower accuracy with the project record than without it, and reconsideration does not consistently improve overall accuracy. Even with a human-corrected alternative available, frontier models struggle with the selected research decisions, while SciΠvot supports systematic evaluation of scientific judgment for LLM agents."

Abstract A new benchmark reveals widespread attribute-object binding failures in multimodal retrievers, and fine-tuning on synthetic text improves cross-modal binding without image supervision.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Retrieval is a fundamental task in multimodal systems: given an image or text query, the goal is to find the most relevant images or texts in a collection, often using a shared embedding space. Reliable retrieval requires these embeddings to capture not only which concepts are present, but also how they are composed. One key compositional challenge is attribute--object binding: encoding which attributes belong to which objects. Existing benchmarks provide a limited view of this capability, typically covering a subset of modality pairings, simple two-object swaps, or small attribute sets. Consequently, it remains unclear how robustly current models preserve attribute--object bindings across different modality pairings and more complex settings. We therefore introduce SABER, a controlled benchmark for evaluating attribute--object binding across all four image and text modality pairings, spanning diverse attribute types and scenes with up to ten objects. Evaluating models on SABER reveals substantial binding failures in both CLIP-style dual encoders and recent MLLM-based embedders. These findings motivate us to ask how binding can be improved efficiently. While multimodal supervision may be effective, collecting suitable images is difficult and costly. Text, on the other hand, is well suited to binding supervision: it is cheap to generate, supports a broader range of attributes and enables precisely controlled hard negatives. We thus finetune an MLLM-based embedder exclusively on synthetic attribute-binding text. Despite the absence of image supervision, the gains transfer to cross-modal and image--image retrieval while preserving general multimodal embedding performance. Our model achieves SOTA results on SABER while also improving on negation and instance-retrieval benchmarks, demonstrating broader benefits of our binding-focused supervision.
[19.10.2026 08:25] Response: {"desc": "Авторы представляют SABER — новый бенчмарк для проверки того, насколько хорошо мультимодальные модели поиска связывают атрибуты с объектами (например, «красный мяч» и «синяя коробка»). Оказалось, что и CLIP-подобные dual encoder, и современные MLLM-based эмбеддеры часто ошибаются в таких связках. Чтобы улучшить эту способность без дорогостоящих изображений, модель дообучают только на синтетическом текстовом датасете с контролируемыми hard negatives. Такое дообучение переносится на cross-modal и image-image поиск, сохраняет общую качество эмбеддингов и даёт SOTA на SABER.", "emoji": "🔗", "title": "Текст учит модели правильно связывать атрибуты с объектами без единой картинки"}

Abstract Visual image-generation training can expand pretrained multimodal models' knowledge, with alignment techniques improving text-based access to that visually acquired information.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Unified multimodal models (UMMs) receive image-generation supervision that exposes them to visual information not fully documented in text. Can learning to generate images expand what pretrained models know? We introduce Knowledge Transfer Probing (KTP), a controlled protocol that supplies new knowledge through image-generation training and tests its accessibility through text-only question answering. Across seven knowledge categories and three UMMs, we observe direct but uneven transfer from visual supervision to language. Motivated by these observations, we successively introduce Textual Alignment and Interleaved Image--Text Alignment, which connect generation and answering through shared knowledge and interleaved responses, respectively. Guidance on selected entities can improve knowledge access on other entities without QA supervision, with additional gains from interleaved alignment. Further analysis shows that knowledge correctly reproduced in images is often more accessible through text, although substantial gaps remain between the two forms of access. These findings support visual generation as a source of knowledge beyond textual supervision, and motivate joint investigation of training data design and model architecture to make this knowledge more broadly accessible.

Abstract MOSS-Image is an open diffusion transformer that combines high-fidelity synthesis with intent-aware prompt enhancement to generate text-rich, layout-coherent images, achieving state-of-the-art open-weight results on long-form text and layout benchmarks.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Recent closed-source image generation systems can produce diverse visual content from simple compositions to information-dense graphics, yet open-weight text-to-image models still struggle with dense text and intricate layouts. To narrow this gap, we introduce MOSS-Image, an open generation system that combines high-fidelity visual synthesis with intent-aware composition. First, we pretrain a 5B single-stream diffusion transformer (DiT) from scratch with a carefully designed recipe, enabling bilingual long-form text rendering and precise layout control. Second, we develop an intention-aware agentic prompt enhancement (PE) module that translates diverse user requests into detailed content and layout specifications. Together, they enable MOSS-Image to generate text-rich and layout-coherent images. We further introduce LongLongTextBench (LLTB) for evaluating long-form English and Chinese text rendering, and RealLayoutArena (RLA) for end-to-end layout-rich generation. MOSS-Image achieves state-of-the-art performance among open-weight models on LLTB, with an average text coverage of 96.3%. On RLA, MOSS-Image ranks second in the end-to-end system comparison, while its backbone ranks first among generators evaluated with shared enhanced prompts. We will release the model, training recipe, and evaluation framework to support further research and promote the text-to-image community.

Модель использует агентный модуль улучшения промптов (prompt enhancement), который переводит запросы пользователя в детальные спецификации контента и layout. Abstract OS-Helix is an end-to-end trained multimodal agent that integrates GUI actions and code execution via hybrid supervised and reinforcement learning, achieving substantial gains on desktop automation benchmarks.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Real desktop automation is neither purely visual nor purely programmatic. Agents must understand user-facing interfaces while efficiently manipulating files, scripts, and system state. However, native GUI/code coordination cannot be obtained by prompt-level tool composition. In our preliminary experiments, naively adding a code tool degrades a GUI agent, revealing that GUI/code routing must be internalized through training on high-quality hybrid data. We present OS-Helix, an end-to-end trained multimodal computer-using agent that unifies GUI actions and code execution in one action space. To construct the required supervision, we introduce application-artifact composition, pairing desktop applications with authentic file artifacts to instantiate executable Ubuntu states, and a decoupled instruction/evaluator generation pipeline that produces environment-grounded tasks whose final states can be verified regardless of the solution modality. We cold-start the policy with SFT on filtered hybrid data and further improve it through online RL in live desktop environments. For RL, we propose Step-balanced GSPO, which aggregates importance weight by action step rather than raw assistant tokens to prevent long code actions from dominating heterogeneous-action optimization. On OSWorld-Verified, OS-Helix-27B and OS-Helix-35B-A3B achieve relative improvements of 35.0% and 33.7% over their matched Qwen3.5 hybrid baselines, while OS-Helix-27B-RL further improves by 42.9% to reach 66.44% success. Ablations provide evidence consistent with contributions from learned GUI/code coordination, scalable verifiable supervision, and action step balanced optimization, while demonstrating cross-platform transfer on WindowsAgentArena. Our project homepage is at https://os-copilot.github.io/OS-Helix/.
[19.10.2026 08:26] Response: {"desc": "В статье представлен OS-Helix — end-to-end обученный мультимодальный агент для управления компьютером, который объединяет GUI-действия и выполнение кода в едином пространстве действий. Авторы показывают, что простое добавление инструмента для кода к GUI-агенту ухудшает его работу, поэтому маршрутизация между GUI и кодом должна усваиваться в процессе обучения на качественных гибридных данных. Для этого предложены конструирование данных из пар «приложение–файл» и пайплайн генерации задач с верифицируемыми финальными состояниями, а обучение проходит через SFT и онлайн RL с новым методом Step-balanced GSPO, который агрегирует importance weights по шагам действий. Abstract An event-centric framework evaluates audio-video consistency across complex multi-event scenes by measuring global harmony, temporal synchronization, and attribute alignment via a trainable reward model.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small As joint audio-video generation models become more capable, they can generate audio-visual scenes exhibiting three forms of complexity: multi-event composition, multi-layer audio mixture, and asymmetric cross-modal relations. However, current evaluations largely reuse metrics from earlier simple and unidirectional tasks. They typically evaluate an audio-video clip as a whole and rely on simple predefined relations, making them unsuitable for these increasingly complex scenes. We propose an event-centric evaluation framework that disentangles audio and visual events at the event level, analyzes each event independently, cross-validates relations across modalities, and aggregates the judgments into a coarse-to-fine, three-level consistency metric. The metric assesses global narrative harmony, event-level temporal synchronization, and fine-grained attribute alignment, providing a general formulation for audio-video consistency evaluation across diverse generation settings and cross-modal relations. We instantiate the metric as AVC-Reward, a trainable audio-video reward model. To evaluate the agreement between automatic judgments and human perception across generation paradigms and relation complexities, we construct AVC-Benchmark, which is primarily built from diverse positive and negative samples generated by various types of audio-video generation models. AVC-Reward achieves state-of-the-art alignment with human judgment among automatic methods, provides interpretable rationales that localize failures, and improves the audio-video consistency of a joint generation model when used as an online reinforcement-learning signal. Project page: https://tsuki0512.github.io/AVC-Reward.github.io/
[19.10.2026 08:26] Response: {"desc": "Статья предлагает новую event-centric систему оценки согласованности аудио и видео в сложных сценах с несколькими событиями, слоями звука и асимметричными межмодальными связями. Метод разделяет аудио- и видеособытия, анализирует каждое отдельно и перекрёстно проверяет связи между модальностями. Результатом становится трёхуровневая метрика: глобальная гармония повествования, временная синхронизация событий и выравнивание атрибутов. На её основе создана обучаемая reward-модель AVC-Reward, которая лучше всего согласуется с человеческими оценками и может использоваться как сигнал для reinforcement learning при обучении генеративных моделей.", "emoji": "🎬", "title": "Оценивать звук и видень событиями, а не целиком"}

[19.10.2026 08:26] Claude request. Model: claude-haiku-5-5. Prompt: Read an abstract of the ML paper and return a JSON with fields: 'desc': explanation of the paper in Russian (4 sentences), use correct machine learning terms. Abstract  Evaluation has been central to advances in AI, and it is equally critical to autonomous discovery. Yet scaling autonomous discovery to real-world engineering challenges faces a fundamental bottleneck: the cost of evaluating progress. In real settings, a single true evaluation, such as a full benchmark or serving trace, can take hours, so a search can afford only a handful of true evaluations. We present DuoDiscover, a search system that jointly discovers what to optimize and how to evaluate progress efficiently, through three mechanisms. First, each worker in a fleet of coding agents co-discovers an optimization target and a fast, tailored proxy evaluator, which we call a yardstick. The agent then hill-climbs the yardstick rather than the true evaluation. Second, since search can exploit gaps between a yardstick and the true objective, fidelity tests continually recheck each yardstick's ranking and invalidate any that drift. Third, the fleet is bottom-up, with no central decomposition of the problem. A lightweight orchestrator spawns and terminates agents, briefs running ones, and seeds new agents with others' confirmed work. DuoDiscover enables autonomous optimization from software to chip architecture. We demonstrate DuoDiscover on two repository-level benchmarks and four GPU and TPU kernel suites covering hundreds of kernels. On SWE-fficiency, it doubles the average speedup of human experts. On SOL-Exec, it ranks first on 121 of 235 kernels and in the top three on 212. On the heavily optimized vLLM and SQLite engines, DuoDiscover achieves 1.92times and 1.38times gains over the base code. On a CPU microarchitecture optimization task with evaluations of up to 28 hours, DuoDiscover surpasses both AlphaEvolve-based ArchAgent v2 and the best human design, with 1.65times ArchAgent v2's gain in 16.7 days versus more than 40 days, advancing the frontier of autonomous chip design.
[19.10.2026 08:26] Response: {"desc": "В статье представлен DuoDiscover — система автономного поиска, которая одновременно находит, что оптимизировать, и как эффективно оценивать прогресс. Каждый агент-кодер создаёт собственный быстрый прокси-оценщик («yardstick») и оптимизирует его вместо дорогой истинной метрики, что важно, когда одна оценка может занимать часы. Фидьюсити-тесты периодически перепроверяют ранжирование таких прокси и отбрасывают те, что расходятся с реальной целью. Флот агентов организован снизу вверх: лёгкий оркестратор запускает и останавливает агентов и передаёт им подтверждённые результаты, а на практике система превосходит экспертов и AI-подходы в задачах от ПО до проектирования чипов.", "emoji": "🔍", "title": "Оптимизируй быстрый прокси, а не дорогую оценку, и проверяй его на честность"}

Abstract Dream2Discern improves runtime auditing of multi-agent systems by implicitly augmenting training data through self-guided hard example generation, boosting accuracy without new trajectory verification.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Reliable deployment of LLM-based multi-agent systems calls for runtime auditors that predict terminal outcomes from observed execution prefixes. These auditors typically learn from a limited corpus of trajectories with verified outcomes. Expanding this corpus requires costly execution and verification, yet broader failure coverage alone provides no explicit criterion for identifying prefixes that the current auditor finds difficult to classify. To this end, we propose Dream2Discern, a runtime auditor trained through implicit augmentation guided by its own prediction loss. During Dream, we select perturbations of the auditor's representation operators to increase prediction loss on recorded prefixes, yielding hard latent variants. During Discern, the auditor learns to predict execution outcomes from these variants using the original labels. The variants are regenerated as the auditor learns, turning a limited corpus into a renewable source of hard examples and requiring no additional trajectory collection or outcome verification. Extensive evaluations on AFTraj-2K and StepShield show that Dream2Discern outperforms competitive failure auditors and achieves over 90% trajectory-level weighted accuracy on both benchmarks while balancing prediction accuracy and timeliness. Project page: https://zbox1005.github.io/dream2discern-project/
[19.10.2026 08:26] Response: {"desc": "В статье предлагается Dream2Discern — рантайм-аудитор для мультиагентных систем на основе LLM, который предсказывает итог выполнения по частичной траектории. Вместо сбора новых размеченных траекторий модель использует имплицитную аугментацию: в фазе Dream выбираются возмущения операторов представления, максимизирующие функцию потерь и порождающие сложные латентные варианты. В фазе Discern аудитор обучается предсказывать исход по этим вариантам с исходными метками, а сами варианты регенерируются по ходу обучения. Abstract MorphAct dynamically adapts robot policy parameters via coupled low-rank generators to resolve conflicting updates across interaction contexts, improving vision-language-action performance.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Progress toward general intelligence in the physical world requires scaling robot learning across diverse environments and interactions. However, existing robot policies use shared parameters that remain fixed across interaction contexts. When different operations favor competing parameter adjustments, fitting their mixture can leave individual operations insufficiently adapted. Our atomic-task diagnostics reveal competing update directions in both vision-language and action parameters, with source-improving updates increasing loss on other tasks. This motivates MorphAct, a contextual parameterization framework that dynamically adjusts robot policy parameters as interaction contexts change. Two coupled generators produce context-dependent low-rank updates for the vision-language and action modules. Their coupling follows the policy's computational dependency: vision-language updates shape the representations that condition action-side parameter generation. Both generators are trained jointly through the native action objective, without operation labels or expert-adapter targets. MorphAct improves performance across three VLA backbones on LIBERO and Meta-World, with gains of up to 16.30%. In real-world evaluation, it achieves gains of up to 14% over π_{0.5}. Ablations support the benefits of contextual parameter generation and representation coupling.
[19.10.2026 08:26] Response: {"desc": "В статье представлен MorphAct — фреймворк контекстной параметризации для robot policy, который динамически подстраивает параметры модели под меняющийся контекст взаимодействия. Авторы показывают, что обновления, полезные для одной задачи, конфликтуют с другими, поэтому общие фиксированные параметры VLA-моделей плохо адаптируются к разным операциям. Два связанных генератора выдают низкоранговые обновления для vision-language и action модулей, причём обновления vision-language части формируют представления, на основе которых генерируются параметры action части. Abstract Belief-Trajectory Energy measures how inputs alter layerwise predictions in language models, yielding signals for task difficulty, review detection, and model attribution.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Large language models (LLMs) progressively revise their predictions across Transformer layers, yet we typically observe only the final output, discarding the trajectory through which it is formed. We introduce Belief-Trajectory Energy(BTE), a model-grounded measure that characterizes an input through the layerwise predictive revisions it induces in a model. By mapping intermediate states into a shared predictive space, BTE provides a principled measure of belief change that can be summarized as either a scalar or a structured depth profile. Theoretically, we show that local BTE corresponds to predictive revision under the Fisher-Rao geometry, while the sequence of revisions captures information beyond the initial-to-final belief change. Empirically, scalar BTE provides a model-relative signal of difficulty across diverse reasoning tasks, while richer BTE representations support human-LLM review detection and fine-grained generator attribution, reaching up to 0.998 macro-AUROC and 95.6% eight-way attribution accuracy. Further analysis shows that BTE develops throughout pretraining and is selectively reshaped by targeted training, demonstrating that the resulting measurement reflects what the scoring model has learned. Together, our results establish belief trajectories as a principled model-grounded signal and suggest a broader perspective in which learned models can themselves serve as instruments for characterizing the data they process. More demonstrations can be found at https://yingjiahao14.github.io/BTE-web/.
[19.10.2026 08:26] Response: {"desc": "Статья представляет Belief-Trajectory Energy (BTE) — метрику, которая описывает, как входной текст меняет предсказания LLM от слоя к слою в Transformer. Промежуточные состояния проецируются в общее пространство предсказаний, и изменение убеждений измеряется как скаляр или как структурированный профиль по глубине. Теоретически локальный BTE соответствует предсказательной ревизии в геометрии Фишера–Рао, а последовательность ревизий несёт информацию, не сводимую к изменению от начального к финальному предсказанию. Эмпирически BTE позволяет оценивать сложность задач, детектировать тексты, написанные человеком или LLM, и атрибутировать генератор (macro-AUROC до 0.998, точность 95.6% в восьмиклассовой атрибуции).", "emoji": "🧭", "title": "Траектория убеждений LLM как сигнал о входных данных"}

Paper text to classify:

"Abstract Belief-Trajectory Energy measures how inputs alter layerwise predictions in language models, yielding signals for task difficulty, review detection, and model attribution.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Large language models (LLMs) progressively revise their predictions across Transformer layers, yet we typically observe only the final output, discarding the trajectory through which it is formed. We introduce Belief-Trajectory Energy(BTE), a model-grounded measure that characterizes an input through the layerwise predictive revisions it induces in a model. By mapping intermediate states into a shared predictive space, BTE provides a principled measure of belief change that can be summarized as either a scalar or a structured depth profile. Theoretically, we show that local BTE corresponds to predictive revision under the Fisher-Rao geometry, while the sequence of revisions captures information beyond the initial-to-final belief change. Empirically, scalar BTE provides a model-relative signal of difficulty across diverse reasoning tasks, while richer BTE representations support human-LLM review detection and fine-grained generator attribution, reaching up to 0.998 macro-AUROC and 95.6% eight-way attribution accuracy. Further analysis shows that BTE develops throughout pretraining and is selectively reshaped by targeted training, demonstrating that the resulting measurement reflects what the scoring model has learned. Together, our results establish belief trajectories as a principled model-grounded signal and suggest a broader perspective in which learned models can themselves serve as instruments for characterizing the data they process. More demonstrations can be found at https://yingjiahao14.github.io/BTE-web/."

Abstract Replacing a learned embedding table with a frozen binary code table reduces model size and parameters while maintaining or improving performance on several benchmarks, and enables faster CPU inference with a ternary body.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small In a small language model the vocabulary is the single largest part. In Gemma 3 270M the embedding table holds 168M of 268M parameters, and because quantisation methods leave the embedding in 16-bit, it is also most of the file once the rest of the model is compressed. We ask what that table actually buys. We replace it with one frozen table of 512-bit codes, 16 MB, with no trainable parameters, derived once from a pretrained embedding by principal-component projection and sign binarisation, and we train the same Gemma-shaped body from scratch on the same 2.05B tokens with and without it. The learned table buys 0.20 nats of validation loss for 2.6 times the trainable parameters, but on LAMBADA, HellaSwag and PIQA the frozen-table model is better or tied; only ARC-Easy favours the learned table. With a ternary BitNet b1.58 body the whole model is a 46 MB file, still ties the float model on LAMBADA and PIQA, and on a laptop CPU generates 615 to 740 tokens per second in 62 MB of memory, five to six times the speed of the float model's 8-bit GGUF in llama.cpp at a seventh of the memory. The frozen table wins by 1.7 to 2.3 nats on rare words, accepts 10,000 new words after training with a 3% cost where the learned table collapses by 43%, and all of it is released with the weights, the data and every evaluation answer.

Такая таблица не содержит обучаемых параметров, существенно уменьшает размер модели и почти не теряет в качестве на бенчмарках LAMBADA, HellaSwag и PIQA.
